Then `editMessageText` is sent with the new bytes. Because the retire and the append share one commit, the latest unretired entry is never the revision-0 entry again. Taps on the revision-0 challenge, and on the interrupted entry's challenge, stay refused ("card updated — tap again"). This holds whether Telegram applied the interrupted edit or never received it. Each such crash uses one of the 16 card entries; a full set ends the request `error{notification_limit}` (SA-3).
- If assist finishes before any card exists, nothing is edited: the first card is sent at revision 1.

**E49 — Terminal-edit table (§9.2; edited in r5).**
- One table covers the approval terminals (approved, denied, canceled, expired, error) and the execution views (executing, succeeded, failed, failed_not_started, indeterminate, expired_unexecuted, stale_target), plus the signer-derived views: `expired_unclaimed` ("approved — not executed: nothing claimed it before its signatures expired"), `not_executable` with its reason (`stale_transfer_key`, `reserved_verb`, or `grant_not_live`: "approved by a standing grant that was revoked, replaced or lost before execution"), and `unreported` ("approved and claimed — no execution report arrived; outcome unknown", provisional, §1.5 SA-6.2).
- It uses one vocabulary: `succeeded`, not "completed".
- Every edit derived from an execution report is labelled "client-reported". The signer-derived views are labelled "signer-derived".
- Each display state gets one best-effort edit, recorded in the record's `status_edits` set (§1.5 SA-4, notify step 4). An `unreported` card later replaced by a client report gets the report's edit as a new display state. (r5.1) The order is "edit, then append", so a crash between the two repeats the edit once after the restart ("message is not modified" counts as success): at most once per display state per epoch, never a resent burst. Display states are monotone per record and at most about five are reachable, so the 12-entry set does not fill; if it ever did, further status edits are skipped (they are best effort).
- Approval terminals and the signer-derived views land in A8; edits derived from execution reports land in D1a.

**E50 — Partial sends (§9.2, §11; edited in r5).**
- Honor 429 `retry_after` inside a 60 s total send budget.
- **(r5.1) Any send failure the actor observes live terminally errors the request (K19: "any partial-send failure terminally errors the request and invalidates all vote challenges"; spec §9.2).** That covers a `*tgbotapi.Error` with a 4xx code, a transport error on any part or on the decision card, and the 60 s budget ending. The request's decision becomes `error`, and already-sent parts get a best-effort "INCOMPLETE — NOT APPROVABLE" edit. A card whose send failed has no recorded message ID, so its challenge can never be valid even if Telegram did deliver it (§1.5 SA-6.1); the activate-after-send precedent is `telegram_policy.go:145`.
- `uncertain` is reserved for what recovery derives: a burst or (r5.4) a `first` or `replacement` card entry from another epoch with no outcome (an edit entry completes with `confirmed` and never makes a notification `uncertain`, E48), meaning a crash (or an uncertain store commit) cut the send after Telegram may have accepted a message but before its ID was committed (spec §6.1). Only `/renotify` resends it. r5 mapped a live transport error to `uncertain`, which left the request pending with partial parts and no card for up to 24 h, against K19. The steps are §1.5 SA-4's notify steps 1–2.
- (r5.8, review 9 #2) A failure observed after the owner's stop latch, or caused by its cancellation, is not observed live. It commits nothing and sends no INCOMPLETE edit, and the send it cut is `uncertain` under the next epoch, as after a crash (§1.5 SA-8). The adapter's 429 wait and the 60 s budget select on the context. (r5.8, critique of the fold) "After the latch" is judged by the latch alone: the budget ending while the owner is not stopping is the live failure above, however the budget is built (SA-8 step 4).
- A test pins the worst-case part count: 24 KiB at 4× escaping is about 26 parts, under the 40-message limit.

**E51 — Callback durability (§6.1; rewritten in r5).**
- The v2 handler checks that the tap comes from the allowed user, in a private chat whose ID equals `allowed_user_id`, on the recorded card's message ID (`telegram_policy.go:74,306`). For a legacy v1 card delivered to a group-linked chat (E64), the recorded card's chat ID replaces the private-chat condition; the sender and message-ID checks are unchanged. The `v:` branch sits in `handleCallback` after the allowed-user check, next to the `p:` branch (`telegram.go:470-473`), not in `dispatch`. Slash commands follow E64.
- The tap becomes one Vote command (§1.5 SA-4). Its commit is confirmed before the callback is answered, the card edited or any waiter woken (spec §6.1). The record lives in memory in Phase A and in its own file from B1.
- An uncertain commit is a crash of the store (§1.5 SA-8): the tap is answered "not recorded yet — tap again". After the reopen a re-tap is idempotent: if the vote landed, it is inert and the answer reports the stored outcome; if it did not, the vote is applied.

**E52 — Quota naming (§10.1; rewritten in r5).**
- r3–r4.1's `minting` state no longer exists (E21).
- Hosted caps (1000 global, 100 per client) count v1 pending rows plus v2 rows whose class is `live` (§1.5 SA-6.5; hosted has no grants, so `live` means pending, or approved with a signature window still open and no final report yet, r5.1), in both v1 Insert and v2 Submit. The count uses a SQL predicate over the row's slot columns, never the cached `class` column; A10 property-tests that predicate against `approvalplan.Class`. (r5.3, critique pass 1) The predicate reads only `decision_status`, `mint_state` and `final_class`: pending (`decision_status IS NULL`), or approved with `mint_state IN ('none','bytes')` and no `final_class`. Under r5.3 `live` is slot-derived (SA-6.5), so the predicate needs no `now` and agrees with `Class` whether or not Clock has run; Clock's `window_closed` and the Erase are what move `mint_state`. A9 adds the column, and A9 and A10 property-test the predicate against `Class`.

**E53 — Migrations (§10.1).**
- All v0.2 hosted schema goes into **one** migration 7, idempotent in the `applyMigration2` style.
- `policy_migration_test.go:168` currently inserts version 7 as the "future" row; it changes to `VALUES(8,'future',1)`. This is a named bump in A9.
- The ledger is roll-forward only (`migrations.go:379-409`). The release note says an older binary cannot open a DB that has migration 7.

**E54 — Local caps, retention and signature erasure (§10.2; rewritten in r5).**
- The store is **one securestate file per record** (§1.5 SA-3), not r3–r4.1's single `approvals.json` snapshot plus `approvals-tombstones.json`. Tombstones and report stubs are withdrawn: a shed record keeps its header in place (§1.5 SA-9).
- **Caps** (§1.5 SA-9): ≤100 live records per principal and ≤256 live in total, ≤512 KiB per encoded record (r5.1; all `queue_full` before any notification); ≤512 records with a full payload and ≤32 MiB of payload ((r5.5) reserved, not current: each full-payload record counts at `approvalplan.R1Reservation`, its whole-life worst case, from Submit until Shed or Delete, §1.5 SA-9); ≤65 536 records in total. The live caps refuse. The payload and count caps first reclaim, oldest first, by Shed and Delete commands (each its own one-record commit), and refuse with `queue_full` only when nothing is reclaimable. (r5.5) So an admitted record's later transitions never exceed the budget and are never refused for it.
- **Signature bytes are erased** (r5.3) by Clock at `claim_end` (the latest signed step's window end, `last_end`, once claimed; (r5.3, critique pass 1) while unclaimed it is `first_end`, where Clock writes `window_closed` instead; §1.5 SA-1 rule 3.3) or when a final report is accepted, whichever comes first; the header keeps the mint digest. They are **never** erased at mint, which the repeat Claim depends on. The executor never journals signatures (§1.5 SA-3).
- **Commit cost.** Every commit writes one file of at most 512 KiB encoded (r5.1), with file and directory fsync (`internal/securestate/store_linux.go:370-438`), plus securestate's directory fsync before each transaction (`:221-233`). The cost no longer grows with the number of records, which r4.1's whole-snapshot rewrite did. B1's stress test reports p99 commit latency against the 250 ms gate, and B1 also measures startup load at 65 536 records (§3 B1).

**E55 — Executor state directory (§10.3; edited in r5).**
- *Evidence:* no state directory exists today; the MCP uses the XDG config root (`src/mcp/cmd/sshgate-mcp/main.go:235-244`).
- *Resolution:*
  - The job records go in `<configRoot>/executor/`, a 0700 securestate directory, one file per job (§1.5 SA-3 R2).
  - The socket and singleton lock go in `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/sshgate/`: the directory is 0700 and the socket 0600.
  - Fix the cross-reference in §10.3 so it points to §7.1.

**E56 — Bounded capture (§7.1, §10.3).**
- The SSH client buffers output without limit (`client.go:151-153`), and `capOutput` truncates only after the fact.
- Async uses bounded writers: 1 MiB per stream per step and 8 MiB per job, with markers counted against the budget. (r5.1) These caps are on raw output bytes. The job record stores bodies as `[]byte`, which canonical JSON encodes as base64 (4/3), and every storage bound, reservation and budget is computed on the encoded size (§1.5 SA-3 R2). (r5.2) That includes the non-body part of the record. The draft alone can encode to about 144 KiB (24 KiB of `<`, each escaped to `<`), more than r5.1's fixed 64 KiB allowance. Bodies are never JSON strings: stdout is arbitrary bytes, and `json.Marshal` would replace invalid UTF-8 with U+FFFD, so the stored body would differ from what the server sent.
- Executor await responses are capped at 16 MiB in total. Any further terminal items return `result_withheld` with the hint "await this id alone".
- Eight MiB per job does not bound total storage. The aggregate result budget, admission reservation, result retention and eviction are in E75 (r3).

**E57 — Executor packaging (K10).**
- Async is unavailable on darwin and in MCPB installs: `async=true` returns `executor_unavailable`.
- MCP startup never dials the executor, preserving the `scripts/smoke-fresh-install.sh` contract.
- The docs cover the re-login or `user@` restart needed after the group add (`install.sh:150-157`), and `loginctl enable-linger`.

**E58 — Integration-tag blind spot.** `make vet` and `make test` never compile `//go:build integration` files. STD therefore runs `go vet -tags integration ./...`.

**E59 — Queue metrics (D3; rewritten in r5).**
- Per-view counts and the oldest pending age, reported through the `status` tool (signer probe via a list summary, plus the executor probe) and through hosted structured log lines.
- Also: the outbox backlog (events not yet audited) and its oldest age; live, `awaiting_report`, settled, shed and total record counts against their caps; executor job counts, storage use and reservation; quarantined record and job counts; a `storage_uncertain` flag with the reopen-attempt count; and the start time of the current store epoch. (r5.1) Also: orphan `.tmp-*` files removed at Startup; records found at Startup with stamps more than 5 min in the future; and a store that refused to open, naming the unexpected directory entry. (r5.3) Also: wall steps seen by the tick (count, largest and the time of the last one, SA-1 rule 3.1); r5.1's `clock_regressed` flag and r5.2's clock-break metrics are withdrawn. (r5.2) Also: a store refused because another process holds its directory, naming the directory (SA-1 rule 1); and executor jobs whose report is still owed, with the oldest one's age (SA-9).
- No new HTTP endpoint is added.

**E60 — TTLs (K16, §13).**
- Run, run_batch and reveal: 60 (`run.go:209`, `run_batch.go:94`).
- Revoke: 60.
- Update and transfer: 300.
- One regression test per kind. A TTL is never widened.
- **The signer enforces the table at freeze, on both planes.** A step whose TTL exceeds its route maximum is refused with `invalid_request`: `signed_write` and `signed_reveal` 60, `signed_admin` 60 for `SSHGATE_REVOKE` and 300 for `SSHGATE_UPDATE`, transfer legs 300. Shorter TTLs are allowed. Today the signer caps only at `MaxSigValidity` (`daemon.go:570-573`), and no production code sets `Runner.WriteTTLSec` above 60, so nothing in-tree breaks. v1 is unchanged.

**E61 — Duplicates (K15, §13; rewritten in r5).**
- Duplicate challenges and duplicate votes are inert and audited. **(r5.1) Every one is audited, whatever its decision.** On v2, a re-send by an operator who already voted changes no state and appends one `vote_replay` noise event, same decision or opposite; an authenticated tap or POST refused because the record is resolved or expired, or because its challenge is stale, retired or undelivered, appends one `vote_refused{reason}` noise event (§1.5 SA-4). Noise events are capped by the 32-event fold (`noise_dropped`), so offset-0 redelivery and double taps stay bounded. A hosted MAC challenge used twice by the same operator is such a duplicate. r5 audited only the opposite decision, which left the common case (Telegram redelivery, double taps) unaudited.
- For v1 duplicates, hosted keeps answering 409 "operator already voted" (`human_handlers.go:784`), now from the unchanged `SubmitVote` after the challenge check (E7 step 7). A same-decision duplicate resumes the interrupted vote, as today. (r5.1) `PrepareVote` refuses an opposite decision without auditing and needs no audit for a same-decision duplicate of an audited vote (`sqlitestore/operators.go:354-392`), so E7 step 7 writes one HTTP-layer audit line for each; the engine is untouched. **(r5.8, review 9 #1)** A v1 POST on a resolved row (an N=1 approve followed by a second approve or a deny, or a POST after expiry or cancel) and a v1 POST refused at the challenge check are audited too, by the same inert line (E7 steps 3, 5 and 7). r5.1–r5.7 audited v1 duplicates only while the row was pending.
- **(r5.4) Local v1 duplicates are audited too.** A tap on a local legacy card that does not resolve the request (a duplicate, an opposite replay, a tap after the deadline, a tap on an unknown handle) writes one fail-open audit line and changes nothing (E8b). The tap answers and the v1 response bytes are today's.
- The ledger reads "inert (no second effect), audited".

**E62 — Package placement (no import cycles; edited in r5).**
- *Evidence:* today `pkg/signerkit` imports no `pkg/signerkit/*` subpackage, and `pkg/signerkit/hosted` imports `pkg/signerkit` (`go list`). A7 and A12 make `pkg/signerkit` import `lifecycle`, so `lifecycle` can never import `pkg/signerkit`.
- *Resolution:*
  - `approvalplan` holds `AuditMetadata` (E23's payload) and the whole pure state core of §1.5: the R1 and R2 schemas, `View`, `Apply`, `Next`, `Class`, `Decide`, `JobView`, `JobNext`, the Claim answer function and the notify-step planner, plus the `approvalplan/statetest` harness. All stdlib only. `signerkit.AuditEvent`, `AuditCall` and `AuditVerdict` gain `Approval *approvalplan.AuditMetadata`.
  - `lifecycle` defines its own narrow interfaces: `AuditWriter`, `Minter`, `Notifier`, `LegacyNotifier`, `Freezer`, `SnapshotBuilder` and `XferDigests`. The adapters live in `pkg/signerkit/daemon_approvals.go` (local) and `pkg/signerkit/hosted` (hosted).
  - `lifecycle` must never import `pkg/signerkit`, `hosted`, `sqlitestore`, `modernc.org/sqlite` or `go-webauthn`.
  - `pkg/signerkit/hosted_v2_client.go` (A12) must never import `hosted`. It speaks `approvalwire` JSON.
  - Tests that need both `signerkit` and `hosted` are external (`package signerkit_test`) or live under `tests/`.
  - A1a adds `verify-lifecycle-closure` to the Makefile. It is a no-op while `pkg/signerkit/lifecycle` does not exist; afterwards `go list -deps ./pkg/signerkit/lifecycle/...` must not match `/pkg/signerkit$|/pkg/signerkit/(hosted|sqlitestore)|modernc.org/sqlite|go-webauthn`.

**E63 — Submit returns before notification (§7.1; rewritten in r5).**
- *Evidence:* a Telegram burst can take up to E50's 60 s send budget with 429 retries, while A5's per-call deadline is 30 s. The policy precedent sends synchronously under `policyNotifyMu` (`telegram_policy.go:84-95`).
- *Resolution:*
  - Submit returns as soon as the record is committed. Its notification view is then `not_started` or `not_applicable`.
  - Notification and assist are post-commit effects run by lifecycle-owned goroutines, which `Close` cancels and joins ((r5.8) after §1.5 SA-8's stop latch, so an interrupted effect commits nothing). All card I/O for a record runs in that record's notifier actor (§1.5 SA-4). Assist completion only commits its slot and nudges the actor.
  - Callers see `sending`, `sent`, `uncertain` or `error` through Get and Wait (§1.5 SA-6.3). §7.1's "return only after the signer record is durably known" holds, and `uncertain` is reported explicitly.
  - **`not_started` always has a driver** (R1.8): the actor started by Submit's effect, the tick, and Startup all run notify step 1 (r5.4, critique of r5.4 C2-7) when a resend is due (no burst carries `renotify_gen`, SA-4 step 1), which at freeze is the first burst. A burst interrupted by a restart becomes `uncertain` (another epoch, no outcome) and is resent only by `/renotify`. No path sends a second burst automatically.
  - The A5 30 s Submit deadline therefore never races the send budget. `ErrResponseLost` stays reserved for genuinely lost responses. The A12 proxy follows the same rule.
- **K10's async return.** K10 says `async: true` asks the service "to durably submit/notify and then returns". The executor's `exec_submit` reply (C2) therefore waits, polling Get every 500 ms, until the notification view leaves `not_started`/`sending`, or for at most 65 s (E50's 60 s send budget plus margin). At that bound it answers `sending` explicitly. The signer's own Submit still returns at commit, so this wait never races A5's per-call deadline. (r5.8, critique of the fold) If the executor stops during this wait, the reply ends at once with the committed job (its `request_id` and `JobView`), never `executor_unavailable`, because the job is durable and resumes after the restart (§1.5 SA-8 step 2, C2 item 6).

**E64 — Telegram slash commands (K18, §9.2).**
- *Evidence:* `dispatch` routes only callbacks and `/start` (`telegram.go:404-420`). `/start` does its own `From.ID` check (`:425`). `handleCallback` checks `From.ID` only (`:455`). Poll restarts at offset 0 (`:339`).
- *Resolution:*
  - Every new command (`/pending`, `/deny_all`, `/cancel <id>`, `/renotify <id>`) goes through one gate in a new `telegram_v2_commands.go`, called from `dispatch`. The gate requires:
    - `From.ID == allowed_user_id`;
    - `Chat.Type == "private"` and `Chat.ID == allowed_user_id`;
    - a message that is not forwarded;
    - (r5.3, critique pass 1) **not redelivered, judged without a clock.** r5.1's `Message.Date ≥ process start − 5s` compared Telegram's clock with the signer's wall clock read once at start, and the signer unit is ordered only `After=network.target` (`scripts/install.sh:173-175`), so a boot-time start often reads an unsynced RTC. A start 10 min fast dropped every `/cancel`, `/deny_all`, `/renotify` and `/pending` for the life of the process, refusing K18's force-deny and the only resend of an `uncertain` record; a slow start re-ran commands from before the restart, and a replayed `/renotify` spent one of the record's 8 bursts. Now the cut is the update stream itself. The poller starts at offset 0 (`telegram.go:339`), and its first fetch after Startup uses timeout 0. Every slash command in that batch, and in each following batch until one returns fewer updates than the fetch limit, was queued before this process could read it, and is dropped as redelivered like any other gate failure. Callbacks in those batches are still processed under E51. After that, this condition drops nothing. A command sent in the first moment after start can land in the drained batch and be dropped, as r5.1's 5 s margin also allowed; the operator re-sends it.
  - Any failure: no state change, an audit line, and a reply sent only to the linked DM, never to the requesting chat.
  - **(r5.1) Addressing.** `/cancel` and `/renotify` take a **record handle** (§1.5 SA-2), which `/pending` and every card show. A `request_id` is accepted only when it matches exactly one record (through the request-ID projection); two principals may hold the same ID (E19), so a match on more than one record is refused with "ambiguous id — use the handle from /pending", and nothing changes.
  - **Group-linked chats.** `handleStart` saves `m.Chat.ID` (`:442`), which may be a group. When the linked chat is not the allowed user's private DM, the signer logs a startup WARN with re-`/start` guidance. v2 notifications then fail closed: the record becomes `error`, reason "linked chat is not a private DM". This follows the policy precedent (`telegram_policy.go:73-75`).
  - **Legacy v1 requests in a group-linked chat (r3; r5 wording).** r2 sent them down the direct `Backend` path, which bypassed K3's legacy label, the shared renderer and K15's challenge, and still emitted request-ID callbacks (`telegram.go:558-559`). That exception is withdrawn (E8e). Legacy v1 cards (§1.5 SA-10) go to the linked chat, which is exactly today's v1 audience, through the shared renderer and with a challenge. Their tap check (E51) requires `From.ID == allowed_user_id` and the recorded card's chat and message ID, and drops only the private-chat condition. An existing group-linked operator therefore keeps v1 approvals with no direct-path exception. The queue commands still require the private DM.
  - `telegram.go` `dispatch` is edited by L-display only: A8 adds the gate hook, B2 adds `/renotify`, and D1b adds the rest.

**E65 — All agent- and model-controlled text is neutralized (K2, §9.1, D-3).**
- *Evidence:* `EscapeBytes` makes command text printable ASCII (`src/policyreview/preview.go:60-73`). The reason (K2 rejects only controls and bidi) and the `AssistResult` strings (summary, effects, concerns) may contain any other non-ASCII, including the renderer's own glyphs `⟦ ⟧ ✓ ✗`.
- *Resolution:*
  - A3's `NeutralizeText` renders printable ASCII as is, escapes `\` and controls as `EscapeBytes` does, and shows **every** non-ASCII code point visibly as `\u{XXXX}` (§9.1 "visibly mark non-ASCII").
  - It applies to the reason, every `AssistResult` string, the legacy per-command reason fields (E8c) and `trusted_display` (E33), on both surfaces. The hosted view model carries only neutralized strings.
  - The renderer's reserved glyphs can therefore appear only in renderer-authored text.
  - The A3, A8 and A11 forging corpora cover the reason, summary, effects and concerns as well as commands.

**E66 — Deleted in r5.** r3–r4.1's hosted-proxy ownership index (`reserved`/`accepted` entries, `hosted-owners.json`, reconciliation and its bounds) is gone. Hosted keys every v2 row on `(client_id, sub_principal, request_id)`, and the local proxy sets `sub_principal` from `SO_PEERCRED` (E19), so it forwards statelessly (A12) and another uid's id is the same miss as a missing one (§1.5 SA-6.6). R1.2 and the proxy half of R2.2 are closed by construction.

**E67 — Expiry is a Clock fact, applied first by every command (K12; rewritten in r5 and again in r5.3).**
- (r5.3) `expired` is K12's "sweeper/CAS terminal state". Clock writes decision `{expired, decided_at = request_expires_at, decided_by = clock}` into the empty slot once `now ≥ request_expires_at` (§1.5 SA-1 rule 3.3). Every R1 command except Delete applies Clock first, in its own commit, and the tick applies it each second. So a tap after expiry commits `expired` and is refused in that one commit, and no Claim can mint. Reads show the slot: until a tick or a command commits it, Get may still show `pending` (R-3), and nothing acts on that stale view.
- No read writes (§1.5 SA-1 rule 3): r3–r4.1's lazy-expiry CAS, its five-step lazy order and decision catch-up are withdrawn (STATE-MODEL G6, F-2). (r5.3) r5's "expiry is a view" is withdrawn too: a view-derived expiry needed a memory-only clock mark to stay shown (`STATE-REDESIGN-NEEDED.md`).
- A v2 vote either committed while the decision slot was empty after Clock, so with `ts < request_expires_at`, or never happened, so there is no prepared-vote exception to expiry. Hosted v1 rows: `handleVote` step 3 expires an overdue row through `ExpireLegacy` (E4c) before answering 409. Local v1: today's first-of semantics (E8b).

**E68 — Assist deadline (K6, K8; rewritten in r5).**
- Freeze persists `assist_deadline = frozen_at + min(timeout_sec, 5 s)` in the header.
- (r5.3) The View shows the assist slot, or `preparing` (revision 0) while it is empty. Clock writes `{unavailable, "assist deadline"}` once `now ≥ assist_deadline` (on the tick, or first in any command), and only then does the View show `unavailable` (revision 1, Approve enabled). A provider result is accepted only while the slot is still empty after Clock, so a late one is dropped (K6).
- **(r5.1) One snapshot, one digest (K8).** The `unavailable` snapshot is a pure function of the header: `generated_at = assist_deadline`, reason `assist deadline`, and empty model, prompt-version and input-digest fields (no model produced it). (r5.3) Clock's snapshot is a pure function of the header, and no view synthesizes it: a card, a hosted challenge or a decision event binds its digest only after the slot commits, and no challenge is issued at revision 1 before then. (`{unavailable, "assist busy"}` and a provider failure are committed slots with their own commit time; no Approve-enabled view exists before they commit, so nothing can bind a different digest.)
- **One attempt (K8).** The assist job starts only as a post-commit effect of a new Submit, never from recovery or a tick. A full pool means an immediate `{unavailable, "assist busy"}`, never a queue. (r5.8, critique of the fold) A pool closed by the owner's stop is not full: `TrySubmit` answers `closed`, nothing is committed, and Clock fills the slot at `assist_deadline` (§1.5 SA-8). A job dispatched after its deadline is not started.
- The watchdog is measured from freeze, not from dispatch.

**E69 — Version skew (E13, A12).**
- After A13b every in-tree MCP write needs a v2-capable signer. An MCP updated through the plugin or MCPB, in front of an older system-unit signer, fails every write with `signer_incompatible` until the operator reinstalls the signer as root (`scripts/install.sh`).
- A hosted signer-server without migration 7 answers `/v2/approvals` with 404: the policy plane's frozen 404 or the mux 404. The A12 proxy and the A5 client map a 404 **whose body is not an `approvalwire` error envelope** (`{approval_proto:1, ok:false, error:{code,…}}`) to `signer_incompatible`, never to `not_found` or a timeout. A typed `not_found` envelope from a v2-capable server stays `not_found`.
- **(r5.7, review 8 #2) The proxy's socket answer.** r5–r5.6 left the proxy with no frame for that case: A5's closed set had no `signer_incompatible`, and the client recognized only the legacy local `unsupported kind` and a hosted HTTP 404, neither of which a current proxy sends. `signer_incompatible` is now a member of A5's closed code set. On any v2 kind, when the hosted answer is a non-envelope 404, the proxy answers the caller with exactly `{"approval_proto":1,"ok":false,"error":{"code":"signer_incompatible","message":"hosted signer-server does not serve /v2/approvals; upgrade it (migration 7) before this MCP"}}` (fixed bytes, A5 golden), writes one fail-open local audit row `refused{signer_incompatible}` (SA-7), and forwards nothing else. The A5 client maps that frame to the same typed `ErrSignerIncompatible` as `unsupported kind` and a direct hosted non-envelope 404. It is definitive in SA-5's split (already listed there) and never retried; A13b maps it to the `signer_incompatible` Denial. A hosted server never sends it: it is a local frame, produced only by the proxy.
- The release notes say: upgrade the signer and signer-server first, then the MCP. See §1.3.

**E70 — Goroutine hygiene and test isolation.**
- `src/signer` and `src/signer/backend` run `goleak.VerifyTestMain` (`src/signer/main_test.go:11`, `src/signer/backend/main_test.go:13`), as do `src/mcp` and `src/mcp/ssh`. A4's `Run` therefore cancels the provider call's ctx at the watchdog and joins its goroutine before returning. "Discarding a late response" means its result is dropped; the goroutine is not left running.
- Every process C5 starts gets a temporary `XDG_RUNTIME_DIR` and `XDG_CONFIG_HOME`, so its flock and socket can never collide with the operator's real executor.

**E71 — Reserved verbs are checked on command bytes at every v2 intake (§5, invariants 1 and 3; new in r3, mint wording edited in r5).**
- *Evidence:* the v1 sign path rejects any `SSHGATE_XFER_*` command before grant matching or signing (`daemon.go:548-561`; `isTransferVerb`, `:1520-1522`, keyed on `xferwire.VerbPrefix`). `matchGrant` refuses every `SSHGATE_`-prefixed command, outermost (`:1456-1470`, check at `:1467`; `isAdminVerb`, `:1510-1512`). Both checks read command bytes. The pinning tests are `TestSignPath_RejectsTransferVerb` (`src/signer/daemon_transfer_test.go:296`), `TestGrant_AdminVerbNeverAutoSigned` (`src/signer/daemon_grant_test.go:456`) and `TestSignRequest_AdminVerbMustBeSoleCommand` (`:189`). A v2 step's `route` is requester-supplied (E35), so a route label can never replace these checks. Without them a forged `signed_write` could reach transfer-leg signing with a requester-chosen recipient key, or a grant could authorize gate administration.
- **`approvalplan.ValidateStepBytes(route, command)`** (A1a, stdlib only) runs on every `command_job` step:
  - an `SSHGATE_XFER_` prefix is refused on every route, `unsigned_read` included (`invalid_request`, code `reserved_verb`). Transfer legs exist only inside a `transfer` payload, whose bytes the signer builds (E34);
  - any other `SSHGATE_` prefix is allowed only on route `signed_admin`, and only as exactly `SSHGATE_REVOKE` or `SSHGATE_UPDATE <64 lowercase hex>`, the two admin forms the gate dispatches (`src/gate/cmd/sshgate-gate/main.go:215,223`). That step must be the job's only step (E33);
  - route `signed_admin` with any other command is refused;
  - a `standing_grant` draft whose `exact_allowed_commands` holds any `SSHGATE_` entry is refused.
- **Where it runs.** At both signer intake boundaries, before quotas, notification and grant matching: (1) the local daemon's v2 Submit, both in the native lifecycle freeze and in the hosted-proxy branch before anything is forwarded (A12); (2) hosted `/v2/approvals` Submit freeze (A10). The MCP draft builder (A13a) also runs it so the agent learns early, but the signer never relies on that.
- **Grant matching.** E9's grant path and `matchGrantV2` first refuse, on bytes and outermost, any step with an `SSHGATE_` prefix or route `signed_reveal`, mirroring v1 `matchGrant` (which r5.2's A7 hunk 5 deletes, OQ3 (b); the v2 refusal is unaffected).
- **Mint-time recheck (r5 wording).** The first Claim re-runs the validator on the stored plan inside its mint commit, through the v2 `Minter` adapters (local `signAt`, hosted `SignApproved` in the Claim transaction), and refuses to sign on failure: the mint slot records `{error: reserved_verb}` and Claim answers `materialization_error` (E21). A corrupted record file or DB row therefore cannot reach the master key. The v1 paths and `SignApproved` itself are unchanged.
- **(r5.9, coordinator ruling 2026-10-15, A1b departure 9) Supersedes the mint-slot write above.** A stored plan that fails E71 also fails A1a's frozen record validation, so the core cannot commit a `reserved_verb` mint slot. The first Claim's recheck therefore answers `record_corrupt` and commits nothing; no signature leaves. Hosted's pre-transaction minter rechecks in its own adapter (A10). Acceptance expects `record_corrupt`, not `materialization_error`, for this case.
- The prefixes are `approvalplan` constants. An A7 internal test asserts they equal `xferwire.VerbPrefix` and `"SSHGATE_"`, and that the validator agrees with `isTransferVerb`/`isAdminVerb` over a corpus, so the signer, the gate and the v2 validator cannot drift.

**E72 — Vote durability (K15, §6.1; rewritten in r5).**
- *Evidence:* hosted voting is two-phase today. `PrepareVote` stores an unaudited vote, the audit runs, `MarkVoteAudited` commits it, then the flip; a same-decision retry resumes an interrupted audit or finalization (`sqlitestore/operators.go:354-392`; `approval_engine.go:122-230`), and unaudited votes block every status flip (`sqlite.go:352-360`). r3–r4.1 copied that two-phase shape into v2 and local records and then needed a driver, a resume rule, an expiry exception and a legacy exception for each intermediate state; the vote seam drew a finding in every review round (R1.3, R2.6, R3.2).
- **v2 (local and hosted): a vote is one command and one commit** (§1.5 SA-4). The guards (challenge validity, `operator` role, step-up, View pending after Clock (r5.3), no earlier vote by this operator), the vote append with `ts = now`, and any decision (`decided_at` = the deciding vote's `ts`) commit together. The callback or POST is answered after the commit is confirmed. The audit follows through the outbox (E23), and no signature leaves before it is flushed through the decision.
- There are no prepared, audited or counted vote states, no resume, no tick, worker or startup vote recovery, and no barrier between votes and expiry or cancel. Whichever command commits first under the store lock wins, judged (r5.3) after Clock at its own `now`, and the decision slot is write-once.
- **Hosted v1 rows** keep the frozen engine unchanged, including its same-decision resume. E4c's `ExpireLegacy` and `CancelLegacy` no longer wait for an unaudited vote.
- **Local v1** keeps today's first-of semantics (E8b).
- Withdrawn: r3–r4.1's S1–S4 protocol, `PrepareVoteV2`, `MarkVoteAuditedV2`, `ListUnauditedVotes`, `GetOperatorVote`, decision catch-up, and the worker's v1 audit completion.

**E73 — Retention and the submit window (§6.1, §10.1, §10.2, K11; rewritten in r5).**
- *Evidence:* r2 pruned any terminal, audited record under cap pressure, which could delete an approved record still owed a claim, an execution or a report, or erase a denial's idempotency fact (R1.4). r3 settled a claimed record ten minutes after its window although the command was still running (R2.4). r4's report stub could later be closed as `unreported` over an accepted report (R3.3), and its tombstone answered `pruned` before checking the owner (R3.4).
- **Class** (§1.5 SA-6.5) is a pure view: `live` (pending; approved with a signature window still open and no final report yet, r5.1; or a live grant), `awaiting_report` (claimed, no final report, before `last_window_end + report_horizon`), `settled` (everything else).
- **Shed** reduces a record to its header in place. It is allowed for a settled, fully audited record at retention (local `[approvals] retention`, hosted `--approval-retention`, default 7 d), and under payload pressure, oldest first, also for a fully audited `awaiting_report` record. **Delete** removes a record that is settled, fully audited and past `submit_not_after + 24 h`, once it is past its age limit (local 7 d; hosted `--approval-max-age`, which r5.1 defaults to 0 = never, E23) or under count pressure, oldest first (hosted: only when an age limit is set). Live work is never shed or deleted. (r5.4, critique of r5.4 C1-7) Both ages count from the record's `decided_at`, and "oldest first" orders by it (§1.5 SA-9).
- **A shed record keeps answering from its header:** an idempotent resubmit returns its summary with `pruned: true` (a different draft is `conflict`); Get, Wait and List return the summary with `pruned: true`; Claim answers from the View (signatures were erased before any shed, so `expired`, `execution_reported` or the terminal state); Report is judged against the header's token hash, `claim_count`, decision and final-report summary, so a late report on a shed `awaiting_report` record is still accepted. r3–r4.1's tombstones and report stubs are withdrawn.
- **Submit window.** Every v2 draft carries `submit_not_after`, inside `draft_hash`. At first acceptance `now ≤ submit_not_after ≤ now + 3600 s` (hosted allows 300 s more for clock skew; (r5.2) the local signer and the executor allow 5 s more, the `clock_skew` guard's margin, because the caller read its clock first and a small step back can leave the checker's wall clock below that reading (r5.3: the window is judged at the wall clock, §1.5 SA-1 rule 3.5); beyond it the signer answers (r5.3, critique pass 1) `submit_window_ahead`, which `JobNext` retries, §1.5 SA-1 rule 3.5; the executor's own `exec_submit` check keeps `invalid_request`). A Submit for an id with no record, arriving after its `submit_not_after`, is refused with `submit_window_closed` before anything is stored. An existing record, shed or not, is returned whatever the time. **(r5.2)** For an async call, the MCP sets the field to call time + 3600 s in the `exec_submit` input. It sits inside `input_hash`, and the executor copies it into the draft unchanged; r5.1 had the executor set it at job creation. The synchronous adapter sets it to Submit + 120 s.
- Because a record is deleted only after `submit_not_after + 24 h`, and Submit refuses a new id after its own `submit_not_after`, a retried old draft can never become a second approval (R1.4) (r5.3, critique pass 1) while either store holds the id; after both delete it, §1.5 SA-1 rule 3's R-2 applies. A lost Submit response is safe at any time: the executor resubmits the same draft until `submit_not_after` and then ends `approval_lost` (§1.5 SA-5).
- **(r5.2) The outer hop has the same horizon (STATE-MODEL OR-1).** Under r5.1 the executor minted `submit_not_after` at job creation and deduplicated `exec_submit` only while the job file existed. So after both the job file (30 d) and the signer record were pruned, a replay of the original `exec_submit` created a new job with a fresh window, and the signer took it as a new id. Under a live grant it ran again with no tap. Now the window is fixed by the caller and covered by `input_hash`. The executor refuses a new id whose input window has passed with `submit_window_closed`, before admission and before any signer call (SA-5). The executor and the signer then read one `submit_not_after`, and neither deletes before it plus 24 h, so no hop can turn an old request into a new approval (r5.3) while either store holds the id. After both have deleted it, a step back of more than a day can let its exact bytes in as a new request, which still needs a tap or a live grant (§1.5 SA-1 rule 3, R-2).

**E74 — Executor step durability and recovery (K11, §6.2, acceptance C-4/C-6; rewritten in r5).**
- *Evidence:* the executor-evidence seam drew a finding in every round: a durable result discarded as `indeterminate` (R1.6), a batch resumed after an abort (R2.1), and a durable check-failure result ignored by `claimed` recovery (R3.1). Each came from evidence split across an index and result files, read by per-state recovery handlers.
- **One file per job** (§1.5 SA-3 R2). A step's result commits atomically with its output bodies, and every job fact lives in that one record.
- **(r5.2) Signature windows apply only to signed steps (K16).** A Claim is needed before step k only if step k is signed, or if no step of the job has a marker or a result yet. That second case is the job's first dispatch, which keeps `claim_count ≥ 1` before anything executes, so report acceptance holds (E25). An unsigned step (`unsigned_read`) later in the job runs with no Claim and no K16 check, after the last signed window has closed and after a restart between steps. This is what `run_batch` does today: reads run unsigned whatever the time (`src/mcp/tools/run_batch.go:250-264`: a read keeps its plain command, and only `signedByIdx` entries are wrapped). r5.1 Claimed before every step, so after the last window a repeat Claim answered `expired` and a trailing read became `expired_unexecuted`.
- **Step k** (§1.5 SA-5): the checks run first (a fresh `registry.ReadCurrent`, then K16 when step k is signed, r5.2; (r5.3) K16 on the held (wall, elapsed) pair of SA-1 rule 3.4, and only for the step about to be dispatched); a failure commits result(k) = `stale_target` or `expired_unexecuted` with no marker. Otherwise marker{k, epoch} is committed, (r5.1) K16 is checked again immediately before `Start` (a slow marker commit under store contention must not spend the 2 s margin; a failure here commits result(k) = `expired_unexecuted` beside the marker, and nothing was dispatched), `RunPhased` runs (E28), and result(k) is committed. (r5.4) That second check is `RunPhased`'s pre-start guard, after the dial, handshake and session setup and immediately before `Session.Start` (E28), so neither a slow marker commit nor a slow handshake spends the margin.
- **(r5.1) One driver per job.** `JobNext` runs single-flight per job, in a per-job actor (a keyed mutex plus a nudge flag, like the signer's notifier actors); ticks, waiter nudges and Startup only set the flag. A marker from the current epoch with no result means the step is in flight, and `JobNext` takes no action for that job. A Marker command on an occupied slot answers `already_marked`, and the driver never proceeds to `Start` on that answer. r5 let a tick during a live step re-pick that step as "the first step with no result" and dispatch it again.
- **Recovery is `JobNext` under a new epoch**, the same function that runs live. In order: a job whose stored results end it (any abort status, a stop-policy stop or the last step), or that holds an outcome, is final and is never dispatched again, which covers a check-failure result (R3.1); a marker from another epoch with no result becomes `indeterminate` before any dispatch can be chosen, so a marked step is never re-dispatched (invariant 7); otherwise the job resumes at the first step with no result, under that step's checks and a repeat Claim of the signer's stored set, which is valid only while those exact signatures are (K11 bullet 2). (r5.3) Once Clock has erased the bytes at `claim_end`, a repeat Claim answers `expired` and the step's result becomes `expired_unexecuted`.
- **(r5.3) Held signature sets have a drop point.** The executor drops a held set and its deadlines at the first of: the last signed step's `Start`, a K16 failure, the job becoming final, or process exit (SA-1 rule 3.4).
- **Approval side:** no `signer_ack` → resubmit the same draft (idempotent) until `submit_not_after`, then `approval_lost`; signer view pending → wait; a terminal non-approved view → the job adopts it.
- A job file that fails validation is quarantined as `report_auth_lost`: never dispatched, reported or overwritten (§1.5 SA-8).
- Withdrawn: `attempt_id`, the index/result-file split, the `final` flag, r4.1 recovery cases (a)–(d), `result_corrupt`, and journaled signatures.

**E75 — Storage bounds for every store (§7.1, §10, acceptance C-12; rewritten in r5).**
- One policy per store. The classes, Shed and Delete are §1.5 SA-9; this table is the per-store summary. Hosted quotas keep spec §10.1's meaning (E52).

| Store | Live bound (refused before notification) | History bound | Reclamation | Phases |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Local R1 files | ≤100 live per principal, ≤256 live in total, ≤512 KiB per encoded record (r5.1; `queue_full`) | ≤512 full-payload records, ≤32 MiB encoded payload ((r5.5) reserved: each full record counts at its `R1Reservation` until Shed or Delete, §1.5 SA-9), ≤65 536 records | Shed at 7 d or under payload pressure; Delete per E73 at 7 d or under count pressure; `queue_full` only when nothing is reclaimable | A6 memory, B1 files |
| Hosted `approvals_v2` | the E52 quota (1000 global, 100 per client); each row ≤ the R1 bound (512 KiB encoded) at freeze, which no K19-legal request exceeds (A1a proves it) | full rows ≤ `--approval-max-full-rows` (4096); all rows ≤ `--approval-max-rows` (200 000); both counted inside the Submit transaction | Shed at `--approval-retention` (7 d), also under full-row pressure; (r5.1) Delete only when `--approval-max-age` is set (default 0 = never, E23), then at that age and under row pressure; by the worker and before a Submit that needs room; `queue_full` when only protected rows remain, or when the row ceiling is reached with Delete off | A9, A10 |
| Hosted v1 `requests` | unchanged | not pruned in v0.2: frozen `/v1/audit` exposes all rows (pre-existing, K17) | — | — |
| Executor R2 files | ≤100 live jobs; (r5.2) admission reserves the job's worst-case **encoded record**: its own encoded non-body part, known at admission because the draft is, plus the base64 of its maximum output (≤8 MiB raw) and the per-step body overhead. That stays within `R2Max = ceil(4/3 × 8 MiB) + R2BodyOverhead + R2NonBodyMax`, where A1a derives `R2NonBodyMax` from the worst-case escaped record (SA-3 R2). All of it counts against a 256 MiB budget (`executor_full`) | ≤10 000 job files; orphan `.tmp-*` files removed at store open (B0, r5.1) | bodies removed 7 d after the job ends ((r5.4) `final_at + 7 d`, SA-3 R2), or under budget pressure once delivered ≥24 h ago, never while undelivered; (r5.2) the raw token removed once the report is settled (acked, refused, or never owed) and the bodies are gone; the job file deleted only under SA-9's deletion predicate (final, bodies gone or none, report settled or never owed, past `submit_not_after + 24 h`), and then 30 d after the job ends ((r5.4) `final_at + 30 d`) or earlier under count pressure; await of a body-less job answers `result_evicted` or `result_expired` with the class | C1, C2 |
| Deny-all and cancel snapshots (local D1a, hosted D2) | ≤16 per admin | memory only, 5 min | discarded at confirm, expiry or restart | D1a, D2 |
| Hosted MAC key; local proxy | — | nothing stored | — | A11, A12 |

- **(r5.3) The executor reservation lifecycle (OR-5).** A live job counts against the 256 MiB budget at its admission reservation. Once it is final it counts at `R2FinalCharge` ((r5.5, critique of the fold) its encoded size plus its still-empty post-final slots at their maximum, §1.5 SA-9), and body removal releases the bodies' share. So the budget bounds what live jobs might still write plus what finished jobs actually hold. Read literally, r5.2's text kept every finished job at its reservation and filled the budget at about 95 finished jobs (C2 tests it).
- **Protected work.** Shed never touches a live or not fully audited record. Delete never touches a live, `awaiting_report` or not fully audited record, or one inside `submit_not_after + 24 h`. `queue_full` from a history bound therefore means protected work fills it, and refusing is the fail-closed answer.
- Sustained-load tests: B1 (local files), A10 (hosted rows), C2 (executor budget).

### 1.2 Optional owner amendments (none is built; ratified behaviour is the build default)

The owner ratified K1–K20 unchanged. **Binding rule (r4, restated in r5):** the build implements the ratified K-ruling behaviour everywhere (E26, C3, E8e). An amendment is never a build default. The build carries no flag, constant, config key, schema field, dead branch or test for it, so nothing in the build depends on the answer. Each amendment lands only on the owner's explicit word, as its own named commit with its own tests. r2 built OQ1 and OQ2 as defaults; r3 withdrew that. r4 also removes the last hook r3 left behind, the reserved `requested_validity_seconds` draft field (E26).

**OQ1 — Amend K12 for the blocking adapter: 5-minute validity for synchronous requests.**
- **(2026-10-18) Owner ruling: ADOPTED** (Telegram button `oq1:adopt` on the 10-18 decision card). The proposed amendment below is now the build behaviour, landed as its own named commit(s) per the binding rule: A1a validator (optional `requested_validity_seconds`), signer freeze on both planes (A7 local, A10 hosted), the A13b synchronous adapter sending 300, their tests, and the §1.3 item 11 text.
- *Ratified behaviour, which is what gets built (E26):* a synchronous request gets the configured validity (default 24 h). When the sync wait budget (300 s) ends, the call returns `approval_pending`, the request stays pending, and nothing ever runs for it. A later approval is never claimed, so nothing is minted and its window passes unused (`expired_unclaimed`); the card says so (E49). An agent that ignores the Denial and resubmits puts a second card in front of the human.
- *Proposed amendment (not built):* the synchronous adapter would send a requested validity of 300 s, and the signer would accept `min(requested, configured)` with a floor of 300 s. Synchronous requests would then expire with the call, and `approval_timeout` ("retry once") would stay the only synchronous timeout outcome.
- *If adopted:* about 25 lines. An optional, omit-when-absent `requested_validity_seconds` draft member, which leaves every existing `draft_hash`/`plan_hash` byte-identical (E26); the A1a validator; signer freeze on both planes; the A13b adapter; their tests; and the §1.3 item 11 text.
- *Recommendation (the owner's call; the build does not wait for it):* adopt. It keeps today's terminal 5-minute synchronous semantics and leaves no orphan cards.

**OQ2 — Amend K9 to reject `async` with `reveal`.**
- **(2026-10-18) Owner ruling: ADOPTED, rejection variant** (Telegram button `oq2:reject`). `run`/`run_batch` with `async:true` and `reveal:true` is refused at input validation, pointing to the synchronous call. Its own named commit in C3 with its test; §1.3 item 12 and THREAT-MODEL text in D3.
- *Ratified behaviour, which is what gets built (C3):* K9 adds `async` to `run` and `run_batch` with no exception, and §10.3 lets the executor journal hold command outputs. So `run(async:true, reveal:true)` works. The executor stores the revealed output in its 0600 journal under the E56/E75 caps. Its body is removed 7 d after the job ends, or earlier under budget pressure once it was delivered at least 24 h before (§1.5 SA-9). `await_approvals` returns it repeatably (§7.2), and the live log blanks it exactly as on the synchronous path (`src/mcp/server.go:354`).
- *Proposed amendment (not built):* reject async reveal in v0.2, with input-validation text pointing to the synchronous call; or keep the raw output only until its first delivery, then purge it and keep the metadata.
- *If adopted:* one validation branch in C3 and its test (rejection), or a purge-after-first-delivery rule in C2's retention (purge variant), plus the §1.3 item 12 and THREAT-MODEL text.
- *Recommendation (the owner's call; the build does not wait for it):* reject. A revealed secret should not sit in a durable journal.

**OQ3 — Security, pre-existing, frozen v1: standing grants are not host-bound.** (Not a K-ruling amendment; it decides frozen v1 behaviour under K13.)
- `matchGrant` (`daemon.go:1456-1500`) checks only alias and command, and the signature takes the host the requester supplied (`:1563`).
- Any process in the `sshgatesigner` group can therefore send `{kind:sign, server:<granted alias>, host:<any registered fingerprint>}` and receive an auto-signed, host-bound signature for a *different* server, with no tap. That group includes the agent's own shell (`install.sh:148-157`).
- v2 grants are host-bound (E24), and (r5.1) v1 `sign` never consults a v2 grant at all. So the question covered only grants **created through v1**, and after A13b no in-tree client creates one. The options were:
  - **(a)** leave it as is and document it; or
  - **(b)** once in-tree MCP is on v2, the v1 `sign` path stops auto-signing under any grant. A v1 grant-covered request prompts instead, and the v1 response bytes are unchanged.
- **(r5.2) Owner ruling: (b). It is the build behaviour; (a) is withdrawn as the default.** It is built as the owner's explicit word, as §1.2's rule requires, in its own named commit inside A7 (hunk 5). That commit removes the v1 `sign` grant short-circuit (`daemon.go:604-606`), so every v1 `sign` goes through `legacyApprove` and prompts (E8b, E24). `matchGrant` (`:1456-1500`) loses its only caller and is deleted. `isAdminVerb` and `isTransferVerb` stay, because the lone-admin rule and the transfer-verb refusal still use them, and E71's drift test reads them. v1 `request_grant`, `revoke_grant` and `list_grants` keep their wire bytes and behaviour; a v1 grant is simply never spent. The named test changes are listed in A7.
- **Timing.** "Once in-tree MCP is on v2" holds at every released point. A7 merges before A13b, but no binary is released between them (§0.4; the Phase A end gate runs after A13b), and the in-tree MCP's v1 grant path is exercised only by `tests/integration`, which runs at Phase A end, after A13b. Between those merges an in-tree checkout prompts for grant-covered writes. That interim is fail-closed.
- **Owner-visible:** §1.3 item 33. **Docs:** D3's THREAT-MODEL paragraph records the gap as closed rather than as a residual (§1.4 item 4).

### 1.3 Owner-visible consequences of ratified rulings (FYI, no question)

1. **K13+K19:** synchronous calls with more than 32 steps, any command over 4 KiB (for example a large heredoc config write) or more than 24 KiB in total are now refused with `request_too_large` before any prompt. Changing that would mean amending K19. (r5.2) A write to a server whose `servers.json` entry exceeds the route-snapshot bounds (alias over 64 bytes, host over 253, user over 64, or a character outside the allowed set; A1a) is refused before any prompt too, naming the field.
2. **K3:** every write needs a reason, including grant-covered writes, plus reveal, request_grant, revoke_server, update_gate and transfer. The plugin's slash commands and skills are updated to pass one.
3. **K20 on hosted:** the hosted web UI shows raw command text today (`human_handlers.go:500-505,565-590`). After A11 it shows display-redacted text with hidden-byte counts and the original SHA-256.
4. **K18:** hosted admins are separate accounts. They can cancel and deny-all, but they cannot approve or vote, and they are not policy voters (E6). On local Telegram the single allowed user is both approver and admin. Per-request cancel on Telegram is `/cancel <id>`, not a card button.
5. **E23:** the hosted ordinary audit sink stays a non-durable stderr sink in v0.2 (`signer-server main.go:251`). (r5) The committed `approvals_v2` row is the durable audit fact for v2; the stderr line is its projection. Making the sink durable is a separate decision. (r5.1) So that fact stays visible and kept: the hosted `/ui/audit` view gains a `v2_entries` list, a shed row keeps its decision and votes, and hosted v2 rows are never deleted unless `--approval-max-age` is set (item 29).
6. **E18:** on darwin the local signer keeps v2 records in memory only, so nothing survives a restart. Async is Linux/systemd-user only (K10).
7. **E53:** migration 7 is roll-forward only, so an older signer-server binary cannot open the upgraded DB.
8. **E69, version skew:** after A13b the in-tree MCP needs a v2-capable signer. An MCP updated through the plugin or MCPB in front of an older signer (or a signer-server without migration 7) fails every write with `signer_incompatible` until the signer is reinstalled. Upgrade order: signer and signer-server first, then the MCP.
9. **E16, §9.1:** a v2 command that cannot be displayed faithfully is refused with `request_unrenderable` instead of being shown fully hidden. After the explicit PEM spans this should be practically unreachable.
10. **E64:** an operator whose bot chat is linked to a group, not a private DM, gets v2 approvals only after a re-`/start` in a private chat. v1 approvals keep working in the linked chat, now on legacy cards with a challenge and the legacy label rather than the old direct cards.
11. **K12+K13, synchronous waits (E26):** a synchronous write whose approval does not arrive within about 5 minutes returns `approval_pending` and nothing runs. The card stays live for up to 24 hours, and approving it later does not execute it; the card then says so. OQ1 would change this.
12. **K9+§10.3, async reveal (C3):** `run(async:true, reveal:true)` works, and the revealed output sits in the executor's 0600 job file until its body is removed: 7 d after the job ends, or earlier under budget pressure once delivered at least 24 h before (E75). OQ2 would change this.
13. **K10, async return (E63):** an async call returns once the approval card is sent: normally about a second, at most about 65 seconds under Telegram rate limiting.
14. **E73:** every v2 request carries a submit window of at most one hour. An executor that was down longer than that does not resubmit a request it cannot find; that job ends `approval_lost`, unexecuted.
15. **E7 (r5 wording):** a hosted vote challenge is bound to the browser session that fetched it, and the server's challenge key is regenerated at every restart. Logging out, logging in again, opening the request in another session, or a signer-server restart all mean reloading the request before voting ("view changed").
16. **(r5) Legacy v1 on local Telegram behaves exactly as today** (`telegram.go:595-606,774-795`): the first of a tap and the deadline (`ApprovalWindow`, or the moment the v1 caller disconnects) wins, once. The v1 response, including a tap's approval, is audited after the response and fails open, as today. Only the card changes: the shared renderer, the legacy label and a `v:` challenge. (r5.4) One addition: a tap that does not resolve the request (a second tap, an opposite tap after a decision, a tap after the deadline) now writes one audit line, as K15 asks; its answer and the v1 response are unchanged. r4's deadline CAS and r4.1's audit-first legacy taps are withdrawn.
17. **(r5) Late execution reports:** a command that keeps running long after its signatures expired can report its result for as long as the signer still holds the request's header: (r5.4, critique of r5.4 C1-7) locally until about 7 d after the decision (and never before `submit_not_after + 24 h`); on hosted until `--approval-max-age` is set and passes, so by default the header is kept (item 29). After `report_horizon` (default 24 h) and before such a report, the card and Get show "approved and claimed; no execution report arrived; outcome unknown", provisionally; a later report replaces it. The executor keeps and returns its own result either way.
18. **Hosted ceilings:** hosted keeps at most 4096 full v2 rows and 200 000 rows in all (both flags). When only protected history fills them, new v2 submissions get `queue_full` until the protected horizon passes. (r5.1) With Delete off by default, the 200 000-row ceiling is reached by history too; see item 29.
19. **E28, pre-existing:** today, if a server rejects the SSH exec request for a signed write, the MCP error repeats the whole signed command, signature included (`x/crypto/ssh` `Session.Start`, wrapped at `run.go:376` and `run_batch.go:274`). The port closes this on every signed path.
20. **Withdrawn in r5.** r4.1's item 20 (v1 taps audited first and unable to approve while the audit sink fails) no longer applies; see item 16.
21. **(r5) Grants reach approved-but-unclaimed work.** A write approved by a standing grant is signed only when it is first claimed, which normally happens immediately. If the grant is revoked, replaced by a newer grant for the same alias, expires, or is lost to a signer restart before that claim, the write is not executed: Claim answers `materialization_error` (`grant_not_live`) and the card says so. This is de-escalation; today's grant path signs inline.
22. **(r5) An uncertain local storage commit restarts the lifecycle in place.** The signer stays up, but every standing grant is dropped, exactly as a signer restart drops them today, and taps in that window are answered "not recorded yet — tap again".
23. **(r5) No card reattach after a restart.** The last card that was delivered stays votable after a signer restart. A card or review burst whose send was cut by a crash or an uncertain store commit is `uncertain`, and only `/renotify` sends a new one. (r5.1) A send that fails while the signer is running (a 4xx, a network error, or Telegram rate limiting past the 60 s budget) ends the request as `error` with INCOMPLETE edits, as K19 requires; the agent resubmits. (r5.8, critique of the fold) A signer stop during a send (SIGTERM: an upgrade, `systemctl restart`) is not such a failure: the request stays pending, its review reads `uncertain` after the restart, and `/renotify` resends it (or it expires); a card whose delivery was recorded before the stop stays votable. (r5.4) `/renotify` is remembered once accepted: a signer crash right after it still sends the new review exactly once. A second `/renotify` before that review goes out adds nothing, and one sent while a review is still being sent answers "a send is in progress — try again".
24. **(r5) Deny-all and cancel snapshots live in memory.** A signer or signer-server restart between `/deny_all` (or the hosted snapshot) and its confirmation discards the snapshot; send the command again. Items already canceled stay canceled.
25. **(r5) Accepted residual, at-least-once like today's synchronous calls:** if the MCP process dies after `exec_submit` reached the executor but before it replied, an agent retry creates a second request (a second card). Within one MCP process a lost reply is retried with the same request ID and never duplicates. (r5.1) Under a live standing grant that second request is approved without a tap and runs, so the duplicate executes. C3's `serverInstructions` and the AGENTS.md sweep therefore tell the agent: after an MCP restart or a lost async reply, call `list_pending_approvals` before resubmitting, as the phantom-live grant note already does for `request_grant`. (r5.3) This is owner item OI-4, accepted by the owner on 2026-10-03 (§11.2).
26. **(r5) Hosted v1 expiry and admin cancel no longer wait for an interrupted vote.** The unaudited vote row stays as evidence, and a vote that races the expiry or cancel loses and its signatures are discarded. (r5.1) Each expiry and cancel writes an audit line naming any vote rows left unflipped, and the losing vote's POST answers 409 "request already resolved" instead of a misleading 200 `pending` (E4c, E7 step 7).
27. **(r5.1) A v2 standing grant belongs to the uid that requested it, and only v2 uses it.** It auto-approves only v2 writes from the same local uid (the MCP, the executor and the agent shell share one), and an old (v1) MCP's `sign` never runs under it, or (r5.2) under any grant (item 33). Mixed-version setups, where an old MCP signs under a grant a new MCP requested, now get a tap. `list_grants` shows each uid only its own v2 grants; `revoke_grant(alias)` still revokes every grant for the alias, as today.
28. **(r5.1) Grant-covered write throughput.** Every grant-covered v2 write is now a record that counts as live until it is reported or (r5.3) Clock closes its 60 s window: at most 100 per principal and 256 in total locally, and at most 100 live executor jobs. Synchronous writes report within seconds and free their slot, so ordinary use never notices; more than 100 grant-covered async writes still unreported at once get `queue_full` or `executor_full`. Today grant-covered signing has no cap. B1 and C2 test a burst.
29. **(r5.1) Hosted v2 rows are kept by default.** Hosted v1 rows are never pruned today, and the v2 row is the audit fact, so hosted Delete is off unless `--approval-max-age` is set. At `--approval-max-rows` (200 000) new v2 submissions then get `queue_full`, and the signer-server logs that the ceiling needs raising or a retention needs setting. Shedding (dropping plans and snapshots after 7 d) still runs and keeps the decision and votes.
30. **(r5.3) Clock errors fail closed, and no clock event interrupts anything or refuses a cancel, deny-all or revoke.** (r5.3, critique pass 1) The refusals a clock error can cause are the ones below: an expired request, a closed window, a signature refused on release, and a card tap that must be repeated because the card was just updated. Decisions and signatures are stamped with the signer's wall clock. An expiry is recorded within about a second of falling due and is never undone. Until then a card or `/pending` may still show the request as pending, but any tap, cancel or claim records the expiry first. A standing grant ends at whichever comes first: its wall-clock expiry or its real elapsed duration (on Linux, counting sleep). So neither a clock step back nor a laptop sleep extends one. If the clock steps back, pending requests and approvals not yet claimed stay open longer, by up to the size of the step. Signatures do not, because each carries its own expiry, which the target's gate checks. If the clock jumps forward, whatever falls due in the jump expires at once and stays expired. A request approved while the clock ran more than 5 s fast refuses to sign once the clock is corrected (`materialization_error`). An async job restarted after such a correction ends its next signed step `expired_unexecuted`, and (r5.3, critique pass 1) the rest of the job does not run. Both need a new request. The gate has no future-timestamp check (§1.4 item 5). After a step back of more than a day, an exact replay of a request that both stores have deleted counts as a new request, and still needs a tap or a live grant (§1.5 SA-1 rule 3, R-2). r5.1's `clock_regressed` refusals and r5.2's clock break are withdrawn. (r5.3, critique pass 1) Telegram commands no longer depend on the signer's clock at start: one started with a fast clock no longer ignores `/cancel` and `/renotify` until it restarts.
31. **(r5.1) AI advisory on legacy v1 cards.** Local legacy v1 cards keep today's explainer summary through the shared assist, with alignment "not assessed". Hosted v1 rows show no AI advisory, as today.
32. **(r5.1) Operators name v2 requests by handle.** `/pending`, cards and the hosted pending list show a short record handle; `/cancel`, `/renotify` and the hosted v2 pages take it. A bare request ID still works on Telegram when it is unambiguous.
33. **(r5.2, OQ3 (b), owner ruling) Grants requested by old (v1) clients no longer auto-sign.** An old MCP or script can still request a v1 grant, and the human can approve it; the grant card says it will not auto-sign (A3); `list_grants` shows it and `revoke_grant` clears it. But every v1 `sign` now prompts, grant or no grant, and its response bytes are unchanged. This closes the pre-existing gap where a v1 grant for one server could auto-sign a write bound to a different server's host key (§1.4 item 4). After A13b, in-tree tools use v2 grants, which are host-bound and per uid, so in-tree use is unchanged.
34. **(r5.2) One signer per approvals directory, and one executor per job directory.** A second signer started with a different socket but the same `journal_root` refuses to start and names the directory, the same way a second signer on the same socket refuses today. The executor behaves the same way for its job directory. (r5.2) Both stores must be on a local filesystem. On a default NFS mount the ownership lock cannot be taken, and the signer or executor refuses to start with a log line saying so.
35. **(r5.5) A synchronous write approved while the signer cannot write its audit record ends early, as "expired, not executed".** The signer releases signatures only after the approval is audited. If its audit sink stays down past the first signature window (60 s for an ordinary write), a promptly approved synchronous write returns about a minute after the approval with `expired_unexecuted`, nothing ran, and the agent is told not to resubmit automatically. Only an approval in the last minute of the 5-minute wait still returns `approval_pending` ("approved, but the signer could not release signatures"). A sink that recovers inside the window is retried each second, so the write then runs normally.
36. **(r5.5) The local 32 MiB payload budget is reserved, not measured.** Each pending or recent record counts at the most it could ever grow to, from submission until it is shed. Ordinary one-command writes reserve little (A1a checks that 256 of them fit in half the budget, or raises the budget), so the live caps (100 per principal, 256 in total) bind first; a principal that keeps many very large requests pending can make new submissions answer `queue_full` sooner than before, which fails closed. The budget is never exceeded, and no admitted request is refused later for lack of room.

37. **(r5.6) A hosted quorum above 256 is refused at startup, and a vote that cannot change the outcome can be refused when the record is crowded.** The signer-server now refuses to start with `--required-approvals` above 256, the ceiling the policy plane already enforces; today such a value is accepted when enough eligible operators exist. Each v2 request holds up to its quorum plus 64 votes. Approvals that count, and any vote that decides, are always accepted. A vote that cannot change the outcome under the current settings (the requesting account's own vote with self-approval off, or a deny with deny-veto off) is refused with "vote limit reached" only once the request already holds about 63 such votes; it is audited, and the operator can vote again if the settings change. (r5.6, critique of the fold) The startup refusal is a plan choice, not a consequence of a K-ruling, so §11.2 also lists it as an owner item with its built default.

38. **(r5.7) An approved command that comes back with the host's read-jail line is now reported as possibly run.** (r5.7, critique of the fold) This covers every signed `run`, a plain approved write as well as `run(reveal:true)`; r5.7 named only reveals. Today any signed `run` that returns exit 77 with the gate's "read jail unavailable" line says nothing ran (`run.go:402-404`). An approved command may be a write, and its own output can print that line, so after this build the step counts as executed (`executed_ordinals`), the Denial is still `read_jail_unavailable` (ask a human to run `gate doctor`, do not retry), and the message no longer says nothing ran. An unsigned read refused this way still did not run, and its Denial summary is reworded to say so for both cases. AGENTS.md's read-jail line is rewritten to match (A13b). Who it affects: an agent or operator reading an approved command's result on a host whose read jail is down; the advice is the same, only the execution claim is more cautious.

39. **(r5.7, critique of the fold) A mixed batch stops at a read the host's read jail refused.** Today, in a `run_batch` that mixes reads and writes, a read the gate refuses with "read jail unavailable" counts as an ordinary failed read, and the approved writes after it still run. After this build that read is recorded as never started (it did not run), which ends the batch: the later approved writes are reported `not_run` and do not run. A host whose gate cannot confirm its read jail needs a human to run `gate doctor` first, and the writes then need a new request and approval. Who it affects: a mixed batch on a host whose read jail is down. An all-read batch keeps today's path and continues past each refused read.

### 1.4 Pre-existing items needing the owner's word (outside this build)

1. **`dist/gate` is stale against HEAD.** I verified this with a reproducible rebuild: `f4b8b82b…` against the committed `146bf158…`. The cause is the Unit-3 `go.mod`/`go.sum` bumps plus `src/redact` edits in the gate closure. `make verify-dist` passes because it checks only the sidecar, but `.github/workflows/verify-gate.yml` would be red. Regenerating is a release decision. This build never runs `release-gate`.
2. **The darwin signer-server build is broken.** I verified it: `main.go:305` fails with `undefined: signerkit.NewDurableFileAuditSink`, because that sink is linux-only and has no stub. `release.yml` builds signer-server for darwin, so a tag would fail.
3. **The uncommitted `go.sum` change** described in §0.2.
4. **The v1 grant host-binding gap (OQ3). (r5.2) Ruled (b) and closed by this build (A7 hunk 5).** The overclaim is `docs/proposed/feature3-grants-reveal-audit-binding.md:86` ("Server-bound … the gate's host-binding makes a target grant physically unusable on a source"). That holds only if the requester supplies the honest host. `docs/THREAT-MODEL.md` says nothing about grants. D3 corrects feature3 and **adds** a grant host-binding paragraph to THREAT-MODEL. That paragraph says v2 grants are host-bound and per principal, and (r5.2) that v1 `sign` no longer spends any grant, so the gap is closed in releases from this build on and remains only in older signers.
5. **(r5.1) The gate accepts a signature timestamp in the future.** `src/gate/verify.go:82-91` checks only `now < Exp` and `Exp − TS ≤ 300`, so a signature minted while the signer's clock runs fast stays valid for its TTL plus the skew. This is pre-existing (v1 signs at `d.now()`, `daemon.go:1544-1565`), and this build's signer-side rule (§1.5 SA-1 rule 3) stops v2 from widening it. A gate-side `TS ≤ now + skew` check changes the gate bytes, so it belongs with #22 or Phase E and needs the owner's word to schedule; D3 records it in THREAT-MODEL. **(r5.3) The concrete fix**, which closes R-5's "signer fast" case:
   - `VerifySigned` refuses `TS > now + 60 s` with a new `ErrNotYetValid`, which the gate maps to exit 65 through the existing `VerifySigned` error path (`src/gate/cmd/sshgate-gate/main.go:216-225` at `366ba07`).
   - No wire bytes change. The gate binary and `dist/gate` do change, so it rides #22's gate release through `update_gate`, on the owner's word. This build does not make it (`src/gate` is #22's).
   - It refuses signatures from any signer more than 60 s ahead of a target, which are accepted today. The owner may prefer 300 s.
6. **(r5.3) v1 grant expiry runs on Go's monotonic clock, so a laptop's sleep extends a v1 grant.** `expiry := d.now().Add(duration)` (`pkg/signerkit/daemon.go:856`) is checked by `!g.expiry.After(now)` (`:958`, `:1488`), and `d.now()` is `time.Now()` (`:1641-1646`), so both values carry Go's monotonic reading and the comparison uses it. On Linux that reading is `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` (Go 1.27.1 `runtime/sys_linux_amd64.s:270`), which stops during sleep (`time/time.go:54`). So in shipped signers a v1 grant on a laptop keeps auto-signing past its reported `expiry_unix` by however long the machine slept. In this build v1 `sign` spends no grant (OQ3 (b)), so here it only affects what `list_grants` shows. Released versions carry it live. `NowFunc` tests cannot see it. It needs a patch-release decision.

### 1.5 State architecture (r5; binding)

r4.1's durable state contract (SC-0 to SC-7) is withdrawn. Its per-record tables were each sound, but 23 of the 28 state findings across three reviews sat in the agreements *between* its 13 record types, and every fix round added records, writers and intermediate states (STATE-MODEL §0, §7: generators G1–G7). This section replaces it with one architecture, judged from three proposals: **write-once facts, derived state** (Proposal 3), with grafts from Proposals 1 and 2 (§10.2). **It is the single authority for durable state, identity, recovery and retention. An erratum, sub-phase or test that disagrees with it is wrong.** It changes no K-ruling (SA-12). A1 builds it as pure code; A6, A10, B1 and C1 build its stores.

#### SA-1 Five rules

1. **One record per request per owner, committed as one atomic unit.** Local signer: one securestate file per record. Hosted: one `approvals_v2` row; each change is one `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction (the DSN already sets `_txlock=immediate`, `sqlitestore/sqlite.go:192`). Executor: one securestate file per job, with its output bodies inline. No transition writes two records. A cross-record read (grant matching, admission counts, card-handle lookup, the due list) reads an in-memory projection that the same single writer updates inside its critical section, after the commit is confirmed; every projection is rebuilt from the records at startup.
   **(r5.2) One owning process per local store, for the store's whole life.** "Single writer" has to hold across processes, not only within one. securestate's flock is taken per transaction and released after it (`internal/securestate/store_linux.go:194-233`), and the signer's singleton lock is keyed by the socket path (`src/signer/cmd/sshgate-signer-telegram/main.go:195-204`, `cfg.Paths.Socket + ".lock"`). So two signers with different sockets and one `journal_root` could each load a cache. Each would then commit from stale state over the other's committed vote, denial or mint slot, because serialized file replacement is not compare-and-swap. The rule is: before loading anything, the owner opens the store directory once, by `openat(parent, name, O_RDONLY|O_DIRECTORY|O_NOFOLLOW|O_CLOEXEC)` after the same `mkdirat` that `securestate.OpenAt` performs. It takes `flock(LOCK_EX|LOCK_NB)` on that descriptor and keeps the descriptor open until the process exits. Every store open, at Startup and at each reopen after an uncertain commit, is `securestate.OpenDirectory(holderFD, …)`. That function duplicates the descriptor with `F_DUPFD_CLOEXEC` (`store_linux.go:112-113`), which shares the holder's open file description, so the opened store is exactly the locked inode, and the lock survives reopens. flock is per inode, so another path spelling, a symlinked parent or a bind mount of the same directory meets the same lock. securestate never flocks the directory descriptor itself (its per-transaction lock is on the separate `lockFD`), and B0's `ListNames` opens its own fresh descriptor, so neither interacts with the holder. `EWOULDBLOCK` means another process owns the store. This is fatal: the process exits non-zero with a log line naming the directory, as `flockOrFail` does for the socket today. **(r5.2) The ownership flock is taken once, at process start, and never again.** A reopen goes through the retained, already-locked descriptor and never calls `flock`, so there is no reopen-time `EWOULDBLOCK` case. Any other `flock` error (`EBADF`, `ENOLCK`, `EOPNOTSUPP`, `EINVAL`) is also fatal, with a log line naming the directory and saying the store must be on a local filesystem. On NFS, `flock` is emulated with whole-file `fcntl` locks, and an exclusive one needs a descriptor open for writing (flock(2), "NFS details"), which a directory descriptor never is. So a store on a default NFS mount refuses to start rather than run without ownership (§1.3 item 34). A lock file beside the store would not cover a bind mount of the store directory, and one inside it would change B0's store grammar, so the directory descriptor stays the lock. This applies to the local signer's store (B1) and the executor's job store (C1). Hosted needs nothing here: it keeps no cache, and every command re-reads inside its own `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction (SA-8). It uses no new securestate method and adds no file.
2. **A record holds only:** an immutable header; write-once slots (the first committed writer wins, and nothing overwrites a slot); append-only bounded sets, whose members may carry their own write-once fields; monotone counters; and a payload that can be shed.
